TOPSIS based multi criteria QSPR modeling of antibiotics using graph theoretic indices
Authors: Atef F. Hashem, Muhammad Farhan Hanif, Amber Shafiq, Ebraheem Alzahrani
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
This paper focuses on the structural features of antibiotic drugs based on topological descriptors defined according to the M-polynomial framework. Degree-based indices, i.e., Zagreb, Harmonic, Forgotten, etc., are calculated and used as molecular descriptors for the purposes of quantitative structure property relationship (QSPR) modeling. For prediction accuracy measurement, the regression models of cubic and power types are utilized and the cubic type is better chosen with larger determination coefficient and lower standard error. In addition, multi criteria decision making methods, such as TOPSIS and SAW, are applied to rank the antibiotics by computed descriptors and physical properties. Objective feature importance is guaranteed by the entropy weighting scheme. This combined approach shows the power of integrating QSPR modeling, graph theoretic indices and entropy based decision analysis for antibiotic screening that provides useful information for drug discovery and optimization.

Introduction

A vertex is a basic building block of a graph. It signifies a single object in the system being represented. In a social network, for instance, every person could be a vertex. An edge is a line between two vertices, indicating a link or a connection between the two. In the social network example, a friendship between two individuals could be an edge. The degree of a vertex is the count of edges that are connected to it1. If a vertex is attached to three other vertices, then its degree is three. In directed graphs, where we give directions to the edges, we also speak of in-degree (edges pointing to a vertex) and out-degree (edges pointing away from a vertex). The order of a graph is the number of vertices in the graph. So, when a graph contains 10 vertices, its order is 10. The size of a graph is the number of edges in the graph. If a graph contains 15 edges, its size is 15.

Graph theory aids in solving complicated problems like determining the minimum path between two points, identifying cycles, and optimizing routes2. It is the study behind numerous algorithms in GPS directions, internet data routing, and artificial intelligence. Knowing fundamental concepts like vertex, edge, degree, order, and size opens the doors to examining and working with networks in the precise and meaningful manner we need to3. Chemical graph theory is a specific branch of graph theory that uses mathematical concepts of graphs to model and examine chemical structures. In chemical graph theory, molecules are modeled as graphs in which the vertices are the atoms and the edges the chemical bonds. This abstraction permits the chemists and the mathematicians to investigate the properties of the molecules using mathematical techniques.

A molecule’s shape can be depicted by a molecular graph in which the atom connectivity is the focus and not the atom positions in space. In a chain of hydrocarbons, a carbon atom will be a vertex, and the carbon-carbon bonds are edges. Hydrogen atoms are at times left off in simplified graphs, traditionally in skeletal formulas. A significant concept in chemical graph theory is the degree of a vertex, which is the atom’s number of bonds. As an example, the carbon atom in methane (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$CH_4$$\end{document}) will have a degree of four, indicating its four single bonds to the hydrogen atoms. The order of a molecular graph is its number of vertices (atoms), and its size is its number of edges (bonds). These simple characteristics serve to define molecular formulas and can be applied to algorithms that forecast molecular stability or behavior4. Chemical graph theory also encompasses the use of topological indices, which are numbers derived from a molecular graph that relate to chemical properties including boiling point, stability, and reactivity. Some examples of topological indices are the Wiener index, the Randic index, and the Zagreb indices.

In general, chemical graph theory closes the gap existing between chemical and mathematical fields of study. It provides a useful framework to examine molecular structure, predict chemical properties, and even design new medicines5. By representing molecules by graphs, researchers are able to apply logical and computational methods in order to understand chemical behaviour. Topological indices are quantitative numbers derived from a molecular structure graph. They are responsible for identifying the crucial shape, connectivity, and branching of a molecule, and are commonly applied in chemical graph theory, quantitative structure–activity relationships, and quantitative structure–property relationships. They enable the prediction of the chemical, physical, and biological properties of compounds without conducting experiments.

In a molecular graph, vertices depict atoms and edges depict chemical bonds. A topological index is a quantity calculated from the graph’s topology, that is, how the atoms are connected and not the actual 3D shape of the molecule6. These types of indices are particularly useful in cheminformatics and computational chemistry. One of the oldest and best-known topological indices is the Wiener index, which was first proposed by chemist Harry Wiener in 1947. It is the sum of the minimum path-lengths of all the pairs of vertices in a molecular graph. It has been applied to predict the boiling points and other physicial properties of the alkanes. Another index that is frequently encountered is the Randic index, and it uses the degrees of the attached vertices in order to measure the branchiness of a molecule8. Molecular branchiness and degree of stability are also investigated using the sum of products and squares of the vertex degrees, which are the Zagreb indices.

Topological indices are also frequently employed as input variables in machine learning models of drug design, toxicity prediction, and property calculation9. They are preferred due to ease of calculation and the potential to express intricate molecular characteristics in a single figure. In short, topological indices convert the structure of a molecule into meaningful numerical data10. They are potent tools in theoretical chemistry, which allow chemists to investigate and predict the behaviour of a molecule using mathematics and graph theory analysis11. QSPR (Structure Property Relationship by Quantitative method) is a computer-based technique that is applied to predict the chemical, physical, and biological properties of a molecule based on its structural characteristics11. Hasani et al.12 applied a set of topological indices to develop QSPR for the acrylate drugs used against pyelonephritis. The MATLAB-based analysis they performed showed good predictive capabilities for relating the molecular structure to the properties of the drugs. Hasani and Ghods13 investigated drugs for the kidney infection via entropy diagram weighted with topological indices. This work underlined the effectiveness of MATLAB programming for using it in finding valid QSPR predictions. It is a highly effective tool in domains like drug development, material chemistry, and environmental chemistry, facilitating the study of how molecular structure relates to different properties14.

Iqbal et al.15 evaluated antiarrhythmia drugs have used QSPR modeling and MCNDA. The research provided an excellent structure to link the structural features to the therapeutic effectiveness. Qin et al.16 have integrated graph-theoretic descriptors with machine learning based methods for prediction of molecular properties of therapeutics for bladder cancer. The results obtained demonstrated that hybrid methods prove to be useful for the accurate determination of drugs. Ahmed et al.17 proposed an advanced QSPR for the analysis of NSAIDs with machine learning with the use of molecular descriptors and molecular modeling of profens. Their work established the utility of data-driven data analysis as predictive endpoints in pharmacological studies. Huang et al.18 studied the drugs for glaucoma with the help of QSPR models created with XGBoost and regression methods. This study demonstrated the promising role of machine learning for drug evaluation accuracy improvement and building confidence.

The principle of QSPR is that the molecular structure is responsible for the behavior and properties of a compound. QSPR, through the use of mathematical models, correlates quantitative measures of molecular structure, including topological indexes, electronic properties, and spatial parameters, to measurable properties like boiling point, solubility, viscosity, melting point, or toxicity19. In order to carry out a QSPR analysis, a database of molecules of known characteristics is initially gathered together. Each molecule is subsequently characterized by a series of numbers referred to as molecular descriptors, which can include topological indices (for example, Wiener or Randic indices), molecular weight, surface area, and more20. These descriptors are subsequently input to construct a predictive model, normally carried out using methodological approaches like linear regression, support vector machines, or neural networks. After the model is validated and trained, it can predict the properties of novel molecules that have not undergone experimental testing. It helps scientists screen and design compounds quickly and efficiently, thereby conserving time and resources21. Validation of QSPR models should be rigorous in order to meet the requirements of accuracy and reliability. Cross-validation and the employment of test sets are typical validation procedures. An appropriately composed QSPR model can bring in-depth knowledge of molecular activity and guide compound prioritization towards synthesis or testing22. The molecular graph structure is represented graph-theoretically by G, and its vertices lie in the set V(G) and edges in E(G). Topological index functions and their associated mathematical polynomial operators.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d_x(w(x,y))&= x \frac{\partial w(x,y)}{\partial x}, \quad d_y(w(x,y)) = y \frac{\partial w(x,y)}{\partial y}, \\ E_x(w(x,y))&= \int _0^x \frac{w(t,y)}{t} \, dt, \quad E_y(w(x,y)) = \int _0^y \frac{w(x,t)}{t} \, dt, \\ \Psi _k(w(x,y))&= x^k w(x,y), \quad J(w(x,y)) = w(x,x). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Eye illnesses like conjunctivitis, glaucoma, dry eye syndrome, and allergic reactions represent common entities that have considerable impact on visual comfort and overall well being. To treat these afflictions, a variety of pharmacologic agents find application, i.e., antihistamines, vasoconstrictors, anti-inflammatory agents, and antibiotics. Such substances tend to be formulated to be topically applied as drops or ointments in order to achieve local action with minimal systemic impact. The following section outlines some of those drugs that find frequent application in ophthalmology, emphasizing structural features and therapeutic use.

Acoramidis is a small molecule drug designed to stabilize the tetramer structure of the TTR. Its chemistry permits it to bind to certain sites of the TTR protein, inhibiting dissociation to monomers that misfold and give rise to amyloid fibrils. This structural stabilization is crucial in treating transthyretin amyloidosis, a progressive disease of heart and nervous system. Acoramidis does not destroy TTR but rather preserves its correct structure24. The drug is at present undergoing studies to assess its effectiveness and safety. The drug has the potential to slow disease progression in impacted patients.

Azelastine belongs to the second generation of antihistamines with its complicated aromatic structure featuring a phthalazine ring. The structure of its molecule enables it to block histamine H1 receptors specifically, and thereby prevent allergic reactions. It is prescribed in nasal sprays and eye drops to treat allergic rhinitis and conjunctivitis. Azelastine has rapid action and provides sustained action with less drowsiness than older antihistamines25. In addition to blocking histamine H1 receptors, it stabilizes mast cells, and this reduces histamine and other mediator release. Its dual activity contributes to its effectiveness in the treatment of allergic states.

Analog of prostamide, Bimatoprost is a man-made compound that acts like endogenous prostaglandins. Its prostaglandin backbone is modified, and this makes it effective at enhancing aqueous humor outflow in the eye. This gives it its ability to treat patients with glaucoma or ocular hypertension by decreasing intraocular pressure. Bimatoprost also induces eyelash growth and is widely used in cosmetic treatments26. Topical application to the eyelashes or around the eye, depending on its intended use, is common. Administration of the drug takes place by targeting prostaglandin receptors to increase fluid outflow of the eye.

Butylene glycol is an organic, simple alcohol with two hydroxyl groups, which provide its moisturizing benefits. Its molecular structure enables it to draw and hold water, making it useful as an effective humectant in cosmetic formulations. Butylene glycol is also a solvent and texture mediator that enhances feel and absorbency. Butylene glycol is widely used in numerous types of cosmetics, such as lotions, creams, and serums27. It is good to use because of its non-greasy finish and ability to be used with other cosmetic agents. It is mostly safe, except for mild irritation to sensitive individuals.

Cimetidine is an H2-receptor antagonist with an imidazole ring that is central to its mechanism of action. The structure of the imidazole ring is responsible for blocking histamine at H2 receptors lining the stomach to reduce acid secretion. Cimetidine is prescribed to treat peptic ulcers, acid reflux, and associated gastrointestinal disease. Cimetidine was one of the earliest drugs of its kind and greatly enhanced the treatment of ulcer disease28. Cimetidine can interact with liver enzymes and impact the metabolism of other medications. Though newer treatments have arisen, it is still an effective option in some clinical scenarios.

Cromoglicic acid, otherwise cromolyn, possesses an unusual polycyclic structure that stabilizes mast cells. This keeps histamine and other inflammatory mediators from being released in cases of allergic reaction. It is prescribed as a preventative treatment of asthma, allergic rhinitis, and conjunctivitis. Cromoglicic acid does not offer immediate symptomatic relief like antihistamines do, but works well when administered regularly29. Its form is widely available as nasal sprays, inhalants, and drops for the eyes. Its safety profile can be used long-term and can be given to children and sensitive patients.

Ertapenem is an imidazolyl carbapenem antibiotic featuring a beta-lactam ring attached to a five-member ring. The structure is designed to interfere with and kill bacteria by inhibiting synthesis of its cell walls. Ertapenem is active against many types of bacteria, although it lacks coverage of some hospital-acquired organisms such as Pseudomonas. It is used to treat complicated infections of many types, including those of the intra-abdomen, pneumonia, and urinary tract infections. Given intravenously, it possesses an unusually high half-life, making it possible to dose just daily30. Ertapenem is reserved for severe to advanced infections to forestall resistance.

Levocabastine is a highly selective antihistamine with a complicated aromatic structure that enables it to have high affinity to H1 receptors. It finds application mostly in form of drops and sprays for treating allergic conjunctivitis and rhinitis31. By inhibiting histamine at receptors, it gives immediate relief to itching, reddening, and sneezing. The drug has local action with minimal absorption and side effects systemically. Levocabastine can be used in treating both intermittent and perennial allergies. Its action is focused and can be applied in both children and adults.

Melatonin is a naturally occurring hormone with an indole ring structure, synthesized from the amino acid tryptophan. Melatonin’s function is to regulate the sleep-wake cycle by binding to melatonin receptors in the brain32. Melatonin is used as an oral supplement to treat insomnia, jet lag, and disorders of the circadian rhythms. Melatonin possesses antioxidant and immune-modulating activity and has stimulated further investigation of additional therapeutic applications. Melatonin is well-tolerated and can be given in many forms. Its indole ring structure enables it to cross the blood-brain barrier and to be effective rapidly.

Miglustat is an iminosugar molecule that has a structure similar to glucose and is thus able to block enzymes involved in glycosphingolipid synthesis. This renders it successful at treating type 1 Gaucher disease and type C Niemann-Pick disease. By decreasing abnormal lipid formation, miglustat controls symptoms and retards disease advancement. Miglustat is given by mouth and can enter the brain and is thereby effective against neurological symptoms. Side effects of this drug include gastrointestinal upset and loss of weight33. It is prescribed when enzyme replacement therapy is inappropriate.

Minoxidil is a derivative of piperidine initially designed as an oral antihypertensive drug. Its pyrimidine ring structure opens vascular smooth muscle potassium channels, resulting in vasodilation. Topical application causes stimulation of hair follicles to increase hair growth in those with androgenetic alopecia. The mechanism by which minoxidil leads to hair regrowth is unknown, but improved circulation to the scalp seems to be involved. Minoxidil is dispensed as both solution and foam to be applied daily. Primary side effects consist of scalp irritation and excess hair growth elsewhere on the skin. Olopatadine is an antihistamine and mast cell stabilizer with tricyclic structure that blocks H1 receptors and inhibits histamine release. The drug is used widely in drops and sprays in the form of medication to alleviate allergic rhinitis and conjunctivitis symptoms34. The drug quickly reduces itching, redness, and swelling. Its dual mechanism of action provides immediate as well as preventive allergy relief. The drug is well tolerated and effective when used long-term.

Propylene Glycol Propylene glycol is a diol compound containing two hydroxyl groups that provide it superior moisture-retention abilities. Propylene glycol is used extensively by the cosmetic, pharmaceutical, and food industries as a solvent, humectant, and stabilizer. Its compact molecular size enables it to penetrate skin tissue and increase the uptake of other active agents. Propylene glycol improves spreadability and texture in lotions and creams as well. Propylene glycol is generally harmless but can be mildly irritable to sensitive skin35. Its adaptability has made it an integral ingredient in numerous personal care items.

Tetryzoline is an imidazoline derivative with a molecular structure that permits selective activation of alpha-adrenergic receptors of the eye and nasal mucous membrane. Its structural design is effective at producing vasoconstriction, thereby decreasing congestion and redness by constricting blood vessels. Tetryzoline is widely applied in over-the-counter nasal sprays and drops as well as other ophthalmologic preparations for relieving temporarily tissue irritation of the eye or nasal congestion36. Its action occurs quickly and is effective in providing symptomatic relief of limited duration. Prolonged use can cause rebound congestion or irritation. The medication is generally safe to use when prescribed at low doses.

Trazodone is a triazolopyridine derivative with an intricate ring system that enables it to act as a serotonin antagonist and reuptake blocker (SARI). Its structure permits modulation of serotonin in the brain, thereby relieving depression and anxiety symptoms. It is also off-label used as an sedative to treat sleeplessness due to its sedative properties. Its dual mechanism of action, against both serotonin and histamine receptors, results in sedation37.

Feng et al.38 developed MRNDR, a multihead attention-based drug development recommendation network that incorporates molecular and pharmacological knowledge representations, which improved the accuracy for drug repurposing tasks. Along these lines, Zhang et al.39 proposed MvMRL (Multi-view molecular Representation Learning), an end-to-end multi-view molecular representation learning method for molecular property prediction, which fully exploits the complementary structural and chemical viewpoints to enhance the predictive performance. In another context, Li et al.40 in a rat model using biliary drainage, analyzed the pharmacokinetic interaction between Warfarin and Senkyunolide I, showing biologically-relevant changes in drug metabolism and highlighting risks in co-administration in a clinical setting. Further, Peng et al.41 reported a systematic profiling of chemical constituents in Dayuanyin decoction by employing UPLC-QTOF-MS in combination with molecular networking, thus facilitating systematic identification of functional ingredients and promoting the research of TCM decoction.The drug is orally administered and is usually well tolerated, though drowsiness or dizziness can be caused by it. It is generally used when antidepressant and sleep-inducing action is required. We denote chemical structure with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_i$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2,...15$$\end{document} and molecular structure with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$MG_i$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2,...15$$\end{document}. Physicochemical properties.

Main results

In this section, We calculate and analyze topological indices of selected drug molecules. Such indices form the input to regression models, where molecular properties are predicted and compared to observed values by means of illustrations. Degree-based bond partitions of eye drug molecules.

Theorem 1

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1=Acoramidis$$\end{document}, we have the M- polynomial\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(G_1,x,y)= & 5{x}{y}^3+5{x}^2{y}^2+8{x}^2{y}^3+4{x}^3{y}^3. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

We can see that degree-based partition of the graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1$$\end{document} gives the following number of edges:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|E_{(1,3)}|=5, |E_{(2,2)}|=5, |E_{(2,3)}|=8, |E_{(3,3)}|=4$$\end{document}The M-polynomial of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1$$\end{document} denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M(G_1, x, y)$$\end{document} can be calculated by using Eq. (1), Table 1 and the edge partition.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(G_1,x,y)= & \sum _{1\le 2}m_{12}(G){x}{y}^2+\sum _{1\le 3}m_{13}(G){x}{y}^3+\sum _{2\le 2}m_{22}(G){x}^2{y}^2+\sum _{2\le 3}m_{23}(G){x}^2{y}^3+\sum _{3\le 3}m_{33}(G){x}^3{y}^3\\= & 5{x}{y}^3+5{x}^2{y}^2+8{x}^2{y}^3+4{x}^3{y}^3\\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Theorem 2

The various indices of the acoramidis graph G are given by

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M_1(G_1) &=104, M_2(G_1)=119, mM_2(G_1)=4.69, ReM_3(G_1)=596, H(G_1)=9.53, \\ F(G_1)&=266, AZ(G_1)=166.46, ISI(G_1)=24.35, SDD(G_1)=52\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

The M-polynomial obtained in Theorem 1, Tables 1 and 3 is used to calculate the indices as explained below.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (d_x+d_y)M(G_1,x,y)= & 20{x}{y}^3+20{x}^2{y}^2+40{x}^2{y}^3+24{x}^3{y}^3=M_1(G_1,x,y),\\ (d_xd_y)M(G_1,x,y)= & 15{x}{y}^3+20{x}^2{y}^2+48{x}^2{y}^3+36{x}^3{y}^3=M_2(G_1,x,y),\\ \frac{1}{d_xd_y }M(G_1,x,y)= & \frac{5}{3 }{x}{y}^3+\frac{5}{4}{x}^2{y}^2+\frac{4}{3 }{x}^2{y}^3+\frac{4}{9}{x}^3{y}^3=mM_2(G_1,x,y),\\ (d_xd_y)(d_x+d_y)M(G_1,x,y)= & 60{x}{y}^3+80{x}^2{y}^2+240{x}^2{y}^3+216{x}^3{y}^3=ReM_3(G_1,x,y),\\ \frac{2}{d_x+d_y }M(G_1,x,y)= & \frac{5}{2 }{x}{y}^3+\frac{5}{2 }{x}^2{y}^2+\frac{16}{5 }{x}^2{y}^3+\frac{4}{3}{x}^3{y}^3=H(G_1,x,y),\\ (d_x^2+d_y^2)M(G_1,x,y)= & 50{x}{y}^3+40{x}^2{y}^2+104{x}^2{y}^3+72{x}^3{y}^3=F(G_1,x,y),\\ \frac{d_xd_y}{d_x+d_y} M(G_1,x,y)= & \frac{15}{4}{x}{y}^3+5{x}^2{y}^2+\frac{48}{5}{x}^2{y}^3+6{x}^3{y}^3=AZ(G_1,x,y),\\ (\frac{d_x}{d_y }+\frac{d_y}{d_x})M(G_1,x,y)= & \frac{50}{3 }{x}{y}^3+10{x}^2{y}^2+\frac{52}{3 }{x}^2{y}^3+9{x}^3{y}^3=ISI(G_1,x,y).\\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Degree-based topological indices for eye drug molecules.

Rgression models

Regression models were used in this study to investigate correlations between topological indices and drug compound molecular properties. Linear and nonlinear models, such as cubic and power models, were used to detect varying trends in the data. The models provide quantitative measures of the indices prediction power. The goodness-of-fit was tested by using standard statistical metrics.

The cubic model is employed to model nonlinear relationships between topological indices and molecular properties. The model uses a third-degree polynomial function, and it can grasp more intricate trends in the dataset. The fitting of the cubic equation enables observation of how molecular descriptors change when structural complexity increases and the detection of trends that linear or quadratic models do not catch. The cubic regression model is given by:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y = a + bx + cx^2 + dx^3$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y$$\end{document} is the dependent or outcome variable, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x$$\end{document} is the independent or predictor variable, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a, b, c, d$$\end{document} represent the regression parameters.

The power model describes the relationship between variables in terms of power-law form, usually in the form of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y = ax^b$$\end{document}. The power model is used when the rate of change between variables is not constant as it scales proportionally. In molecular descriptors, the power model describes how certain indices relate to size or to branching in molecules. The power model is represented as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y = ax^b$$\end{document}, where both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b$$\end{document} are nonlinear regression-determined constants.

To measure the goodness of fit of the models, statistical measures like the correlation coefficient (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R$$\end{document}), coefficient of determination (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}), standard error (SE), F-statistic (F) and the p-value are used. The values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} reflect the goodness of fit and proportion of variance accounted by the model, respectively. The higher value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} implies good fit of the model. The SE gives an idea of precision of predictions and the overall significance of the regression model is tested by the F-statistic. The lower p-value (preferably \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<0.05$$\end{document}) implies that observed relationship is significant.Correlation coefficient: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R = \frac{\sum (x_i - \bar{x})(y_i - \bar{y})}{\sqrt{\sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2 \sum (y_i - \bar{y})^2}}$$\end{document}Coefficient of determination: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2 = 1 - \frac{\sum (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{\sum (y_i - \bar{y})^2}$$\end{document}Standard Error (SE): \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SE = \sqrt{\frac{\sum (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{n - k}}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document} is the number of measurements and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document} is the number of parameters of the model.F-statistic: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F = \frac{(R^2 / k)}{((1 - R^2) / (n - k - 1))}$$\end{document}p-value: The p-value is the probability of observing results by chance. The p-value is calculated using the F-distribution of the F-statistic.The regression of different physicochemical properties of drug molecules by using the first Zagreb index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document} as predictor is given in Table 5. The cubic and power models both have strong correlation values (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} between 0.768 and 0.983) and reflect that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document} is an effective descriptor in QSPR studies. Of note is that the cubic model provides slightly improved fits in comparison to the power model in most cases, especially in boiling point (BP), molar refractivity (MR), and polarizability (PO), as evidenced by lower standard errors and higher values of the F-statistic. Figure 2 visually depicts this trend by predicting MR: the cubic model (red line) fits observed values slightly better than does the power model (black dashed line) and captures the nonlinear character of the relationship. This indicates that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}, as sensitive to the degree distribution of molecular graphs, captures the essence of structural features related to electronic and bulk properties.Table 5Statistical parameters and regression models for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M_1(G)}$$\end{document}.PropertyModelsEquationsR\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_E$$\end{document}Fp-valueBPCubic\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.000460 M_1^3 - Correlation coefficient: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R = \frac{\sum (x_i - \bar{x})(y_i - \bar{y})}{\sqrt{\sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2 \sum (y_i - \bar{y})^2}}$$\end{document}

Correlation coefficient: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R = \frac{\sum (x_i - \bar{x})(y_i - \bar{y})}{\sqrt{\sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2 \sum (y_i - \bar{y})^2}}$$\end{document}

Coefficient of determination: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2 = 1 - \frac{\sum (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{\sum (y_i - \bar{y})^2}$$\end{document}

Coefficient of determination: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2 = 1 - \frac{\sum (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{\sum (y_i - \bar{y})^2}$$\end{document}

Standard Error (SE): \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SE = \sqrt{\frac{\sum (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{n - k}}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document} is the number of measurements and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document} is the number of parameters of the model.

Standard Error (SE): \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SE = \sqrt{\frac{\sum (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{n - k}}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document} is the number of measurements and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document} is the number of parameters of the model.

F-statistic: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F = \frac{(R^2 / k)}{((1 - R^2) / (n - k - 1))}$$\end{document}

F-statistic: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F = \frac{(R^2 / k)}{((1 - R^2) / (n - k - 1))}$$\end{document}

p-value: The p-value is the probability of observing results by chance. The p-value is calculated using the F-distribution of the F-statistic.

p-value: The p-value is the probability of observing results by chance. The p-value is calculated using the F-distribution of the F-statistic.

Statistical parameters and regression models for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M_2(G)}$$\end{document}.

Fig. 3Comparison of observed P values with predicted values from cubic (red line) and power (black dashed line) regression models using the topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2$$\end{document} as the independent variable.

Comparison of observed P values with predicted values from cubic (red line) and power (black dashed line) regression models using the topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2$$\end{document} as the independent variable.

The statistical outcomes of the modified second Zagreb index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$mM_2$$\end{document} can be observed in Table 7. Among all indices investigated, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$mM_2$$\end{document} always returns maximum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} values for descriptors such as MR and PO (with maximum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} of 0.984 and 0.990, both when fitting to cubic and power models, respectively) that show outstanding capability to represent nonlinear trends. The large F-values further confirm the validity of the models. Figure 4 illustrates this capability by way of molecular volume (MV) fit: the cubic curve plots virtually perfectly against observed values, and shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$mM_2$$\end{document} captures both fine-scale multiplicative and branching connection arrangements in molecular structure successfully. This superior predictability capacity can be due to how \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$mM_2$$\end{document} focuses attention onto edge interaction by way of normalization, and in doing so is particularly adept at capturing fine-scale molecular variation. Table 8 presents results of regression analyses using reformulated third Zagreb index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ReM_3$$\end{document} that generalizes classical Zagreb indices and takes both additive and multiplicative degree terms into account. The model statistics reflect superior performance by cubic models with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} values of above 0.91 in most cases and significantly higher values in case of power models, like 0.973 in case of molar refractivity and polarizability. Figure 5 depicts predicted versus observed values of the evaporation rate (EV) where again the cubic model follows the curvature of the data much better and minimizes residual errors compared to the power function. These results highlight the applicability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ReM_3$$\end{document} in encoding both local and global connectivity patterns of molecular graphs and as such a highly informative molecular descriptor to predict molecular behavior in case of different properties.Table 7Statistical parameters and regression models for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${mM_2(G)}$$\end{document}.PropertyModelsEquationsR\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_E$$\end{document}Fp-valueBPCubic\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$7.788021 mM_2^3 - Comparison of observed EV values with predicted values from cubic (red line) and power (black dashed line) regression models using the topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ReM_3$$\end{document} as the independent variable.

Regression results using harmonic index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document} reflecting reciprocal vertex degrees and hence sensitive to sparsity and less-connected vertices appear in Table 9. Strong predictive capability is evident in the models with the cubic model performing between 0.634 and 0.835 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} values, and especially high values of boiling point (BP) and molar refractivity (MR). The cubic model has superior fit compared to the power model in Fig. 6, and both increasing and stabilizing trends of the data are well-captured. Fig. 6Comparison of observed BP values with predicted values from cubic (red line) and power (black dashed line) regression models using the topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document} as the independent variable.

Comparison of observed BP values with predicted values from cubic (red line) and power (black dashed line) regression models using the topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document} as the independent variable.

Table 10 summarizes regression results with Forgotten topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F$$\end{document}, given by sum of squared degrees and emphasizes contributions of high-degree atoms. The cubic model demonstrates strong, consistent fit to all of the properties with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} values of above 0.90 for BP, MR, and PO, and outstanding 0.934 for both MR and PO. Figure 7 shows fit to flash point (FP) where again, cubic model improves upon power model in terms of smoothness and accuracy. Table 11 shows regression results for atom bond connectivity (AZ) index, renowned to emphasize molecular bonding geometry. The cubic model has notably good fit quality, particularly in the case of BP (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2 = 0.935$$\end{document}) and MR (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2 = 0.910$$\end{document}), and the power model performs even slightly better in a few instances (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2 = 0.930$$\end{document} for PO and MV). Figure 8 depicts IR prediction, where both models closely align, albeit with the cubic model again giving a clearer representation of curvature. Comparison of observed IR values with predicted values from cubic (red line) and power (black dashed line) regression models using the topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AZ$$\end{document} as the independent variable.

Statistics in Table 12 using the Inverse Sum Index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ISI$$\end{document} place higher emphasis on low-degree vertices and edge sparsity. Statistical parameters and regression models for ISI(G).

Comparison of observed ST values with predicted values from cubic (red line) and power (black dashed line) regression models using the topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ISI$$\end{document} as the independent variable.

Table 13 and Fig. 10 show regression fits of different physicochemical properties against topological index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SDD(G)$$\end{document} as the independent variable, using both power and cubic models. The cubic models show superior performance in regard to the majority of properties, as reflected by higher \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} and lower standard errors (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SE$$\end{document}) values relative to those of power models. This is illustrated by the cubic model of MR (molar refractivity) with an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} of 0.976 and exceptionally low \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SE$$\end{document} of 0.091 as compared to power model’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} of 0.971 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SE$$\end{document} of 0.091, both of which have p-values that are statistically significant (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$< 0.001$$\end{document}). Introduction to multi-criteria decision making (MCDM)

Multi-criteria decision making (MCDM) is an array of procedures and methods employed to rank and prioritize alternatives considering many, and at times conflicting, factors. Such procedures find extensive application in disciplines like engineering, economics, environmental planning, and policy making, where decisions need to be arrived at against complicated circumstances. The Technique for Order Preference by Similarity to Ideal Solution (TOPSIS) and Simple Additive Weighting (SAW) are among the extensively employed MCDM procedures. The Entropy Method is used to provide objective weighing of factors to ensure impartiality.

TOPSIS (technique for order preference by similarity to ideal solution)

The Technique for Order Preference by Similarity to Ideal Solution (TOPSIS) is a widely employed Multi-Criteria Decision Making (MCDM) technique that ranks an array of alternatives according to relative closeness to the ideal solution. The fundamental principle of TOPSIS is that the best option should be closest to the positive ideal solution (which optimizes benefit factors and minimalizes cost factors) and as far as possible from the anti-ideal solution (which does the opposite). The technique works by normalizing the decision matrix to remove differences in scales, assigning weights to represent relative importance of each criterion, and computing distances of every alternative to both the ideal and anti-ideal solutions. The relative closeness index of every alternative is then calculated, making it possible to rank easily. Its apparent logic and simplicity of computation have made TOPSIS a favored technique in many decision-making applications, ranging from supplier selection to performance evaluation and risk assessment.

In this research, every alternative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i$$\end{document} is an individual eye drug candidate, and every criterion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j$$\end{document} is a topological index (e.g., Forgotten index, Zagreb index, etc.) characterizing structural properties of the drug molecules. The number of alternatives is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m$$\end{document} and the number of criteria (topological indices) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document}. The original (unprocessed) value of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j^\text {th}$$\end{document} topological index of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i^\text {th}$$\end{document} drug is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{ij}$$\end{document}. The notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{ij}$$\end{document} is used to represent the normalized value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{ij}$$\end{document} to remove differences in scales between criteria. The weighted normalized value, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{ij}$$\end{document}, is calculated by multiplying by the weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_j$$\end{document} of the normalized value. The sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{\text {benefit}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{\text {cost}}$$\end{document} denote criteria to be maximized and minimized, correspondingly. The symbols \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^+$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^-$$\end{document} represent the positive ideal solution (best values in any of the criteria) and negative ideal solution (worst values of any of the criteria), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_i^+$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_i^-$$\end{document} represent Euclidean distances to these ideal points. The relative closeness coefficient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i^*$$\end{document} is calculated as how close every drug is to the ideal solution. In the entropy method, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{ij}$$\end{document} is unit proportion of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i^\text {th}$$\end{document} alternative in criterion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_j$$\end{document} is an entropy value reflecting the dispersion of criterion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_j$$\end{document} is its degree of diversification. The final entropy-weight based weight of criterion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j$$\end{document} is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_j$$\end{document}.

TOPSIS rates options according to how far they stand from an optimal positive solution and an optimal adverse solution. Its steps include Step 1: Normalize the Decision Matrix\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{ij} = \frac{x_{ij}}{\sum _{i=1}^m x_{ij}}$$\end{document}.

Step 2: Compute Entropy for Each Index\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_j = -k \sum _{i=1}^m p_{ij} \cdot \ln (p_{ij}), \quad \text {where } k = \frac{1}{\ln m}$$\end{document}.

Step 3: Compute the Degree of Diversification\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_j = 1 - e_j$$\end{document}.

Step 4: Calculate Weights\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_j = \frac{d_j}{\sum _{j=1}^n d_j}$$\end{document}.

These entropy weights can subsequently be used in TOPSIS or SAW to objectively appraise the candidates of eye drugs.

Step 5: Normalize the Decision Matrix

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{ij}$$\end{document} represent the value of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j^\text {th}$$\end{document} topological index of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i^\text {th}$$\end{document} candidate drug. The normalized value is represented as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{ij} = \frac{x_{ij}}{\sqrt{\sum _{i=1}^m x_{ij}^2}}, \quad i = 1, 2, ..., m; \quad j = 1, 2, ..., n$$\end{document}.

Step 6: Compute the Weighted Normalized Matrix

Apply weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_j$$\end{document} to each normalized topological index:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{ij} = w_j \cdot r_{ij}.$$\end{document}Step 7: Identify the Ideal Solutions

Determine the positive ideal solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^+$$\end{document} and negative ideal solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^-$$\end{document} as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^+ = \left\{ v_1^+, v_2^+, ..., v_n^+ \right\} , \quad v_j^+ = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \max (v_{ij}), & \text {if } j \in J_{benefit} \\ \min (v_{ij}), & \text {if } j \in J_{cost} \end{array}\right. }$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^- = \left\{ v_1^-, v_2^-, ..., v_n^- \right\} , \quad v_j^- = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \min (v_{ij}), & \text {if } j \in J_{benefit} \\ \max (v_{ij}), & \text {if } j \in J_{cost} \end{array}\right. }$$\end{document}.

Step 8: Compute Separation Measures

Calculate the Euclidean distances from the ideal and anti-ideal solutions:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_i^+ = \sqrt{ \sum _{j=1}^n (v_{ij} - v_j^+)^2 }, \quad S_i^- = \sqrt{ \sum _{j=1}^n (v_{ij} - v_j^-)^2 }$$\end{document}.

Step 9: Calculate the Relative Closeness\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i^* = \frac{S_i^-}{S_i^+ + S_i^-}, \quad 0 \le C_i^* \le 1$$\end{document}.

Step 10: Ranking Eye drugs can be ranked by decreasing order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i^*$$\end{document}. The highest value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i^*$$\end{document} suggests the best drug candidate with respect to topological indices.Table 14Weight allocation to topological Table 14 depicts the assigned weights for each topological index (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1, M_2, mM_2, ReM_3, H, F, AZ, ISI, SDD$$\end{document}) in the decision-making process. Such relative importance is depicted by the assigned weights of each criterion to assess the performance of drugs employing the method of multi-criteria decision-making, e.g., using TOPSIS. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2$$\end{document} is assigned the highest weight value of 0.12569, followed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ReM_3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F$$\end{document} with weight values of 0.13608 and 0.12053, respectively, indicating that such indices have relatively more influence in the evaluation process. Normalized decision matrix of drugs and topological indices.

Table 16 displays the normalized decision matrix in which each raw value of every topological index for the given drugs is converted to a unified scale. This is to provide comparability across different units and magnitudes. Prior to any weighted aggregation, normalization is very important as it removes any kind of bias due to the scale. For example, the normalized value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document} for Acoramidis is 0.02573, whereas for Cromoglicic Acid is 0.04207. These represent the relative performance of every drug according to each index without using the weights.

Table 17 is the weighted normalized decision matrix obtained by multiplying values from Table 16 by the relevant weights from Table 15. This step weighs both the relative importance of each selected index, along with the performance of the drug on said index. For instance, the weighted normalized value for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document} for Acoramidis is 0.00283. We use these to determine distances to positive ideal and negative ideal solutions in the TOPSIS method. The normalized chart Fig. 11a gives comparative insight into different drugs across equally scaled parameters. In the chart, every axis corresponds to one specific evaluation criterion (e.g., M1, M2, ReM3, etc.), and all the drugs are scaled from the minimum to maximum value across all drugs. This method ensures every criterion has an equal contribution in the visualized form. It does not take the relative weight or importance of each criterion into consideration, which might eventually contribute to less sophisticated interpretation. For example, drugs like Cromoglicic Acid and Epinastine have persistently high performance across the majority of the axes and thus appear prominently in the normalized plot.Table 17Weighted
