Each portion represents the contribution of each drug to the overall decision space according to its \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i^*$$\end{document} score. The graph emphasizes Eratapenem, Cromoglicic Acid, and Levocabastine as top contributors, demonstrating they have high alignment with ideal solution attributes. On the other hand, drugs like Propylene glycol and Butylene glycol take minimal slices, signifying weaker performance. This graphical form supplements the table by providing intuitive insight into the distribution and relative strength of each drug in the multicriteria appraisal.

SAW for eye drug evaluation

The Simple Additive Weighting (SAW) technique is a basic Multi-Criteria Decision Making (MCDM) technique to rank and rate alternatives by computing the weighted sum of their performance regarding multiple criteria. In this research, SAW is implemented to rank eye drug candidates according to several topological indices computed as descriptors of their structures. Each drug is considered as an alternative, and every topological index is considered as a decision criterion. The process starts by scaling values of every topological index to make them comparable. Benefit criteria (which are to be maximized) and cost criteria (which are to be minimized) are treated differently to account for their optimization directions. Subsequently, every criterion is multiplied by an importance weight representing relative importance of every criterion, and these weights can be given subjectively or in an objective manner by using tools like entropy weighting. The total score of every drug is calculated by summing its weighted normalized values at every index. Eye drugs are then ranked according to total scores, and highest indicates maximum promising candidate. SAW technique is preferred due to simplicity, transparency, and interpretability in decision problems. The Simple Additive Weighting (SAW) method is also used to rank eye drugs using their topological indices.

Step 1: Normalize the Decision Matrix\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{ij} = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{x_{ij}}{\max (x_{ij})}, & \text {if } j \in J_{benefit} \\ \frac{\min (x_{ij})}{x_{ij}}, & \text {if } j \in J_{cost} \end{array}\right. }$$\end{document}.

Step 2: Calculate the Weighted Scores\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_i = \sum _{j=1}^n w_j \cdot r_{ij}$$\end{document}.

Step 3: Ranking

Rank the eye drugs according to descending order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_i$$\end{document} with higher values denoting improved performance according to the topological indices’ weighted sum. The entropy method is used to find objective weights for every topological index.Table 20Normalized decision matrix.Drug\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$mM_2$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} Normalized decision matrix.

Table 20 gives the normalized decision matrix employed in the SAW and TOPSIS methods. All criterion values have been made comparative by normalization across different units and scales. This facilitates comparison of drugs against various criteria like efficiency (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2$$\end{document}), safety (H, F), and composite measures like ISI and SDD. Drugs like Eratapenem and Cromoglicic Acid have consistently high values in most criteria, reflecting strong performance. Conversely, drugs like Butylene glycol and Propylene glycol have much lower values in various parameters, reflecting weaker multi-criteria profiles. Weighted normalized decision matrix.

Table 21 presents the weighted normalized decision matrix where each one of the criterion values is multiplied by its corresponding weight according to its relative importance within the decision-making process. This ensures that the more important criteria have higher influence in the final rankings. In the table, Eratapenem, Cromoglicic Acid, and Levocabastine have high weighted scores across many criteria, again asserting their dominance in the top rank. The values in the matrix simply add up to the SAW scores shown in Table 21, offering a transparent method of multi-criteria evaluation.Fig. 13(a) Radar chart of normalized topological indices for various drugs. (b) Pie chart of SAW scores distribution for the drugs.

(a) Radar chart of normalized topological indices for various drugs. (b) Pie chart of SAW scores distribution for the drugs.

The radar Fig. 13 plots the normalized values of different topological indices (M1, M2, H, F, SS, ABC, RI, SC, GA, HZ) for different sets of drugs in one view, offering comparative insight into the nature of the drugs. Each polygonal line corresponds to a different set of drugs, indicating the differences in the topological nature of the drugs. The spread and shape of the polygons enable the identification of the drugs with the higher and lower values across the different indices. Drugs with larger polygons in the radar chart have higher values for most indices, whereas the ones near the center have lower values. This type of visualization helps to get insight into the relative topological nature of the drugs.

The pie Fig. 13 illustrates the distribution of SAW scores among the drugs. Each segment’s size is proportional to the SAW score of the respective drug, indicating its relative preference. Higher SAW scores correspond to larger segments, reflecting better performance or suitability of the drug based on the weighted topological indices. The chart provides a clear, proportional view of how each drug performs relative to others, making it an effective visual tool for comparative analysis.Table 22SAW scores and rankings of drugs.DrugSAW ScoreRankEratapenem0.99561Cromoglicic acid0.94222Levocabastine0.85973Azelastine0.79304Olopatadine0.70485Bimatoprost0.69536Trazodan0.69257Acoramidis0.57428Minoxidil0.43249Tetryzoline0.420810Melatonin0.414511Cimetidine0.411212Miglustat0.385813Butylene glycol0.112614Propylene glycol0.094215

SAW scores and rankings of drugs.

Table 22 presents the ultimate SAW (Simple Additive Weighting) scores and ranks for all drugs. It is calculated by summing the weighted normalised values for all criteria under each alternative. Eratapenem has the highest rank with the maximum SAW score of 0.9956, followed by Cromoglicic Acid and Levocabastine, proving to be the best under all weighted criteria. At the lower extreme are Propylene glycol and Butylene glycol, reflecting low overall performance. Table 22 is the conclusive summary of the SAW approach, making it appropriate for evidence-based selection of the best performing drugs under multi-criteria consideration.

Conclusion

For this study, there was extensive QSPR analysis to derive predictive relationships for different physicochemical properties of compounds from sets of topological indices. Multiple regression models, such as cubic, were examined to ensure exhaustiveness in exploring the correlation of topological descriptors with target properties. The findings showed some of the models to have higher predictive accuracy than others, indicated by better \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} values and small standard errors. Importantly, the study pointed out the importance of particular topological indices, which maintained perfect correlations with the target variables and revealed much about the structural factors affecting the properties. In addition to its fundamental contribution to the understanding of the relationship of molecular structure to properties, the findings of the current research have immediate practical implications for the structural optimization and design of compounds of desired properties. Researchers will be able to predict the properties of new compounds with confidence using the best-performing QSPR models elucidated in the study and simplify the drug development and discovery process. This research demonstrates the potential of QSPR modeling as a useful tool in computational chemistry and opens the door to further researches into even richer molecular descriptors and sophisticated regression methods.
